</td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data"><span style="display:none">(<span class="bday">1779-03-15</span>)</span>15 March 1779<br /><a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a>, England</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">24 November 1848<span style="display:none">(1848-11-24)</span> (aged&#160;69)<br /><a href="/wiki/Brocket_Hall" title="Brocket Hall">Brocket Hall</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hertfordshire" title="Hertfordshire">Hertfordshire</a>, England</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Resting place</th><td class="infobox-data label"><a href="/wiki/St_Etheldreda%27s_Church,_Hatfield" title="St Etheldreda&#39;s Church, Hatfield">St Etheldreda's Church, Hatfield</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Political party</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/British_Whig_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="British Whig Party">Whig</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spouse</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1151524712">.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin2px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-2px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin3px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-3px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-display-ws{display:inline;white-space:nowrap}</style>

   <div class="marriage-line-margin2px">&#8203;</div>&#32;<div style="display:inline-block;margin-bottom:1px;">&#8203;</div>&#40;<abbr title="married">m.</abbr>&#160;<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1038841319">.mw-parser-output .tooltip-dotted{border-bottom:1px dotted;cursor:help}</style><span class="rt-commentedText tooltip" title="3 June 1805">1805</span>&#59;&#32;died&#160;<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1038841319"><span class="rt-commentedText tooltip" title="25 January 1828">1828</span>&#41;<wbr />&#8203;</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Children</th><td class="infobox-data">Stillbirth child<br />George Augustus Frederick<br />A daughter</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Parents</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist">

</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Signature</th><td class="infobox-data"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_Signature.svg" class="mw-file-description" title="William Lamb, 2nd Viscount Melbourne&#39;s signature"><img alt="Cursive signature in ink" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_Signature.svg/128px-William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_Signature.svg.png" decoding="async" width="128" height="29" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_Signature.svg/192px-William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_Signature.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_Signature.svg/256px-William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_Signature.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="304" data-file-height="70" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>William Lamb, 2nd Viscount Melbourne</b>, in some sources called <b>Henry William Lamb</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-cantab_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cantab-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">PC</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_Ireland" title="Privy Council of Ireland">PC (Ire)</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">FRS</a></span> (15 March 1779&#160;&#8211;&#32;24 November 1848) was a <a href="/wiki/Whig_(political_faction)" class="mw-redirect" title="Whig (political faction)">Whig</a> politician who served as the <a href="/wiki/Home_Secretary" title="Home Secretary">Home Secretary</a> and twice as the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a>. 
</p><p>His first premiership ended when he was dismissed by <a href="/wiki/William_IV" title="William IV">William IV</a> in 1834, the last British prime minister to be dismissed by a monarch. Five months later, he was re-appointed and served for six more years, into the reign of <a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen Victoria</a>. He is best known for coaching the Queen in the ways of politics, acting almost as her private secretary. His legacy as prime minister was not favourable, as he had no great foreign wars or domestic issues to handle, and he was involved in several political scandals in the early years of Victoria's reign.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2><p>
In 1779, William Lamb was born in <a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a> to an aristocratic <a href="/wiki/British_Whig_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="British Whig Party">Whig</a> family, and was the son of the <a href="/wiki/Peniston_Lamb,_1st_Viscount_Melbourne" title="Peniston Lamb, 1st Viscount Melbourne">Penistone</a> and <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Lamb,_Viscountess_Melbourne" title="Elizabeth Lamb, Viscountess Melbourne">Elizabeth Lamb</a> (1751–1818). However, his paternity was questioned, being attributed to <a href="/wiki/George_Wyndham,_3rd_Earl_of_Egremont" title="George Wyndham, 3rd Earl of Egremont">George Wyndham, 3rd Earl of Egremont</a>, to whom it was considered he bore a considerable resemblance, and at whose <a href="/wiki/Petworth_House" title="Petworth House">residence</a> he was a visitor until the Earl's death. Lamb was called to Egremont's bedside when Egremont was dying but,<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> nevertheless, stated that Egremont being his father was 'all a lie'.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup></p><figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Hoppner_(1758-1810)_-_William_Lamb_(1779-1848),_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_RCIN_400973_-_Royal_Collection.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/John_Hoppner_%281758-1810%29_-_William_Lamb_%281779-1848%29%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_RCIN_400973_-_Royal_Collection.jpg/160px-John_Hoppner_%281758-1810%29_-_William_Lamb_%281779-1848%29%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_RCIN_400973_-_Royal_Collection.jpg" decoding="async" width="160" height="200" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/John_Hoppner_%281758-1810%29_-_William_Lamb_%281779-1848%29%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_RCIN_400973_-_Royal_Collection.jpg/240px-John_Hoppner_%281758-1810%29_-_William_Lamb_%281779-1848%29%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_RCIN_400973_-_Royal_Collection.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/John_Hoppner_%281758-1810%29_-_William_Lamb_%281779-1848%29%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_RCIN_400973_-_Royal_Collection.jpg/320px-John_Hoppner_%281758-1810%29_-_William_Lamb_%281779-1848%29%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_RCIN_400973_-_Royal_Collection.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1318" data-file-height="1650" /></a><figcaption>Portrait by <a href="/wiki/John_Hoppner" title="John Hoppner">John Hoppner</a>, 1796</figcaption></figure><p>He was educated at <a href="/wiki/Eton_College" title="Eton College">Eton</a>, then at <a href="/wiki/Trinity_College,_Cambridge" title="Trinity College, Cambridge">Trinity College, Cambridge</a>, where he was admitted in 1796 and graduated a <a href="/wiki/Master_of_Arts_(Oxford,_Cambridge,_and_Dublin)" title="Master of Arts (Oxford, Cambridge, and Dublin)">Master of Arts</a> in 1799,<sup id="cite_ref-cantab_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cantab-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> and finally at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Glasgow" title="University of Glasgow">University of Glasgow</a> (1799–1801), where he was a resident pupil of Professor <a href="/wiki/John_Millar_(philosopher)" title="John Millar (philosopher)">John Millar</a> alongside his younger brother <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Lamb,_3rd_Viscount_Melbourne" title="Frederick Lamb, 3rd Viscount Melbourne">Frederick</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Admitted to <a href="/wiki/Lincoln%27s_Inn" title="Lincoln&#39;s Inn">Lincoln's Inn</a> in 1797, he was <a href="/wiki/Call_to_the_bar" title="Call to the bar">called to the bar</a> in 1804.<sup id="cite_ref-cantab_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cantab-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Against the background of the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a>, Lamb served at home as <a href="/wiki/Captain_(British_Army_and_Royal_Marines)" title="Captain (British Army and Royal Marines)">Captain</a> (1803) and <a href="/wiki/Major_(United_Kingdom)" title="Major (United Kingdom)">Major</a> (1804) in the Hertfordshire Volunteer Infantry.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He succeeded his elder brother <a href="/wiki/Peniston_Lamb_(1770%E2%80%931805)" title="Peniston Lamb (1770–1805)">Peniston</a> as heir to his father's title in 1805 (and as captain of the <a href="/wiki/Midland_Troop,_Hertfordshire_Yeomanry" class="mw-redirect" title="Midland Troop, Hertfordshire Yeomanry">Midland Troop, Hertfordshire Yeomanry</a>, when he resigned his commission in the Volunteer Infantry<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>) and married <a href="/wiki/Lady_Caroline_Lamb" title="Lady Caroline Lamb">Lady Caroline Ponsonby</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Irish_people" title="Anglo-Irish people">Anglo-Irish</a> aristocrat. After two miscarriages and a stillbirth child, she gave birth to George Augustus Frederick in 1807 and was devoted to him. He was epileptic and mentally handicapped, requiring significant medical care. He died in 1836.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> In 1809, they had a daughter, born premature and lived only a day.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The following year, Lamb was elected to the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">British House of Commons</a> as the Whig MP for <a href="/wiki/Leominster_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Leominster (UK Parliament constituency)">Leominster</a>. For the election in 1806 he moved to the seat of <a href="/wiki/Haddington_Burghs_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Haddington Burghs (UK Parliament constituency)">Haddington Burghs</a>, and for the 1807 election he successfully stood for <a href="/wiki/Portarlington_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Portarlington (UK Parliament constituency)">Portarlington</a> (a seat he held until 1812).<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lamb first came to general notice for reasons he would rather have avoided: his wife had a public affair with <a href="/wiki/George_Byron,_6th_Baron_Byron" class="mw-redirect" title="George Byron, 6th Baron Byron">Lord Byron</a> — she coined the famous characterisation of Byron as "mad, bad and dangerous to know".<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> The resulting scandal was the talk of Britain in 1812. 
</p><p>Lady Caroline published a Gothic novel, <i><a href="/wiki/Glenarvon" title="Glenarvon">Glenarvon</a></i>, in 1816; this portrayed both the marriage and her affair with Byron in a lurid fashion, which caused William even greater embarrassment, while the spiteful caricatures of leading society figures made them several influential enemies. Eventually the two were reconciled, and, though they separated in 1825, her death in 1828 affected him considerably.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_politics">Early politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Member_of_Parliament">Member of Parliament</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Member of Parliament">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomas_Lawrence_(1769-1830)_-_William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_NPG_5185_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Thomas_Lawrence_%281769-1830%29_-_William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_NPG_5185_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg/220px-Thomas_Lawrence_%281769-1830%29_-_William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_NPG_5185_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="264" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Thomas_Lawrence_%281769-1830%29_-_William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_NPG_5185_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg/330px-Thomas_Lawrence_%281769-1830%29_-_William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_NPG_5185_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Thomas_Lawrence_%281769-1830%29_-_William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_NPG_5185_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg/440px-Thomas_Lawrence_%281769-1830%29_-_William_Lamb%2C_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne_-_NPG_5185_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg 2x" data-file-width="666" data-file-height="800" /></a><figcaption>Lord Melbourne by <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Lawrence" title="Thomas Lawrence">Thomas Lawrence</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1820s</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1816, Lamb was returned for <a href="/wiki/Peterborough_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Peterborough (UK Parliament constituency)">Peterborough</a> by Whig grandee <a href="/wiki/William_Fitzwilliam,_4th_Earl_Fitzwilliam" title="William Fitzwilliam, 4th Earl Fitzwilliam">Lord Fitzwilliam</a>. He told <a href="/wiki/Henry_Vassall-Fox,_3rd_Baron_Holland" title="Henry Vassall-Fox, 3rd Baron Holland">Lord Holland</a> that he was committed to the Whig principles of the <a href="/wiki/Glorious_Revolution" title="Glorious Revolution">Glorious Revolution</a> but not to "a heap of modern additions, interpolations, facts and fictions".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He, therefore, spoke against parliamentary reform, and voted for the suspension of <i><a href="/wiki/Habeas_corpus" title="Habeas corpus">habeas corpus</a></i> in 1817 when <a href="/wiki/Sedition" title="Sedition">sedition</a> was rife.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_13-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lamb's hallmark was finding the middle ground. Though a Whig, he accepted the post of <a href="/wiki/Chief_Secretary_for_Ireland" title="Chief Secretary for Ireland">Chief Secretary for Ireland</a> in the moderate Tory governments of <a href="/wiki/George_Canning" title="George Canning">George Canning</a> and <a href="/wiki/Frederick_John_Robinson,_1st_Viscount_Goderich" class="mw-redirect" title="Frederick John Robinson, 1st Viscount Goderich">Lord Goderich</a> on 29 April 1827. Upon the death of his father in 1828 and his becoming the 2nd <a href="/wiki/Viscount_Melbourne" title="Viscount Melbourne">Viscount Melbourne</a>, of <a href="/wiki/Kilmore,_County_Cavan" title="Kilmore, County Cavan">Kilmore</a> in the <a href="/wiki/County_of_Cavan" class="mw-redirect" title="County of Cavan">County of Cavan</a>, he moved to the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a>. He had spent 25 years in the Commons, largely as a backbencher, and was not politically well known.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Home_Secretary">Home Secretary</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Home Secretary">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In November 1830, the Whigs came to power under <a href="/wiki/Charles_Grey,_2nd_Earl_Grey" title="Charles Grey, 2nd Earl Grey">Lord Grey</a>. Melbourne was Home Secretary. During the disturbances of 1830–32 he "acted both vigorously and sensitively, and it was for this function that his reforming brethren thanked him heartily".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_13-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> In the aftermath of the <a href="/wiki/Swing_Riots" title="Swing Riots">Swing Riots</a> of 1830–31, he countered the Tory magistrates' alarmism by refusing to resort to military force; instead, he advocated magistrates' usual powers be fully enforced, along with special constables and financial rewards for the arrest of rioters and rabble-rousers. He appointed a special commission to try approximately 1,000 of those arrested, and ensured that justice was strictly adhered to: one-third were acquitted and most of the one-fifth sentenced to death were instead transported.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_13-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> There remains controversy regarding the hanging of <a href="/wiki/Dic_Penderyn" title="Dic Penderyn">Dic Penderyn</a>, a protester in the <a href="/wiki/Merthyr_Rising" title="Merthyr Rising">Merthyr Rising</a> who was then, and is now, widely judged to have been innocent. He appears to have been executed solely on the word of Melbourne, who sought a victim in order to 'set an example'.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> The disturbances over reform in 1831–32 were countered with the enforcement of the usual laws; again, Melbourne refused to pass emergency legislation against sedition.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_13-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Melbourne supported the 1834 prosecution and <a href="/wiki/Penal_transportation" title="Penal transportation">transportation</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Tolpuddle_Martyrs" title="Tolpuddle Martyrs">Tolpuddle Martyrs</a> to <a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia">Australia</a> for their attempts to protest against the cutting of agricultural wages.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister">Prime Minister</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Prime Minister">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Government">Government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Whig_government,_1830%E2%80%931834" title="Whig government, 1830–1834">Whig government, 1830–1834</a> and <a href="/wiki/Second_Melbourne_ministry" title="Second Melbourne ministry">Second Melbourne ministry</a></div>
<p>After Lord Grey resigned as Prime Minister in July 1834, William IV was forced to appoint another Whig to replace him, as the Tories were not strong enough to support a government. Melbourne, who was the man most likely to be both acceptable to the King and hold the Whig Party together, hesitated after receiving from Grey a letter from the King requesting Melbourne to visit him to discuss the formation of a government. Melbourne feared he would not enjoy the extra work that accompanied the office of Premier, but he did not want to let his friends and party down. According to <a href="/wiki/Charles_Greville_(diarist)" title="Charles Greville (diarist)">Charles Greville</a>, Melbourne said to his secretary, Tom Young: "I think it's a damned bore. I am in many minds as to what to do". Young replied: "Why, damn it all, such a position was never held by any Greek or Roman: and if it only lasts three months, it will be worthwhile to have been Prime Minister of England&#32;&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Sic" title="Sic">sic</a></i>&#93;." "By God, that's true", Melbourne said, "I'll go!"<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Compromise was the key to many of Melbourne's actions. He was personally opposed to the <a href="/wiki/Reform_Act_1832" title="Reform Act 1832">Reform Act 1832</a> proposed by the Whigs and later opposed the repeal of the <a href="/wiki/Corn_Laws" title="Corn Laws">Corn Laws</a>, but he reluctantly agreed to both.<sup id="cite_ref-Cecil,_David_1954_p.422_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cecil,_David_1954_p.422-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Melbourne was also a strong supporter of slavery. He called <a href="/wiki/Slavery_Abolition_Act_1833" title="Slavery Abolition Act 1833">Britain's abolition of slavery</a> a "great folly" and said that if he had had his own way (as opposed to what many Whigs wanted), he would "have done nothing at all!"<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>William IV's opposition to the Whigs' reforming ways led him to dismiss Melbourne in November. He then gave the Tories under Sir <a href="/wiki/Robert_Peel" title="Robert Peel">Robert Peel</a> an opportunity to form a government. Peel's failure to win a House of Commons majority in the resulting <a href="/wiki/1835_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1835 United Kingdom general election">general election</a> (January 1835) made it impossible for him to govern, and the Whigs returned to power under Melbourne that April. This was the last time a British monarch attempted to appoint a government to suit his own preferences.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Blackmail">Blackmail</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Blackmail">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:A_large_sheep_with_the_head_of_a_man_perches_on_the_knees_of_Wellcome_V0050210.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/A_large_sheep_with_the_head_of_a_man_perches_on_the_knees_of_Wellcome_V0050210.jpg/220px-A_large_sheep_with_the_head_of_a_man_perches_on_the_knees_of_Wellcome_V0050210.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="172" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/A_large_sheep_with_the_head_of_a_man_perches_on_the_knees_of_Wellcome_V0050210.jpg/330px-A_large_sheep_with_the_head_of_a_man_perches_on_the_knees_of_Wellcome_V0050210.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4d/A_large_sheep_with_the_head_of_a_man_perches_on_the_knees_of_Wellcome_V0050210.jpg/440px-A_large_sheep_with_the_head_of_a_man_perches_on_the_knees_of_Wellcome_V0050210.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3011" data-file-height="2357" /></a><figcaption>Cartoon about the affair by John Doyle. Credit: Wellcome Collection</figcaption></figure>
<p>The next year, Melbourne was once again involved in a sex scandal. This time, he was the victim of attempted blackmail from the husband of a close friend, the society beauty and author <a href="/wiki/Caroline_Elizabeth_Sarah_Norton" class="mw-redirect" title="Caroline Elizabeth Sarah Norton">Caroline Norton</a>. The husband demanded £1,400, and when he was turned down, he accused Melbourne of having an affair with his wife.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> At that time, such a scandal would have been enough to derail a major politician and so it is a measure of the respect that contemporaries had for his integrity that Melbourne's government did not fall. The King and the <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Duke of Wellington</a> urged him to stay on as prime minister. After Norton failed in court, Melbourne was vindicated, but he stopped seeing Caroline Norton.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nonetheless, as the historian <a href="/wiki/Boyd_Hilton" title="Boyd Hilton">Boyd Hilton</a> concludes, "it is irrefutable that Melbourne's personal life was problematic. Spanking sessions with aristocratic ladies were harmless, not so the whippings administered to orphan girls taken into his household as objects of charity".<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Queen_Victoria">Queen Victoria</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Queen Victoria">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Melbourne was Prime Minister when <a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen Victoria</a> came to the throne (June 1837). Barely eighteen, she was only just breaking free from the domineering influence of her mother, the <a href="/wiki/Princess_Victoria_of_Saxe-Coburg-Saalfeld" title="Princess Victoria of Saxe-Coburg-Saalfeld">Duchess of Kent</a>, and her mother's adviser, Sir <a href="/wiki/John_Conroy" title="John Conroy">John Conroy</a>. Over the next four years, Melbourne trained her in the art of politics, and the two became friends: Victoria was quoted as saying she considered him like a father (her own had died when she was only eight months old), and Melbourne's son had died at a young age.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Melbourne was given a private apartment at <a href="/wiki/Windsor_Castle" title="Windsor Castle">Windsor Castle</a>, and unfounded rumours circulated for a time that Victoria would marry Melbourne, 40 years her senior. Tutoring Victoria was the climax of Melbourne's career: the prime minister spent four to five hours a day visiting and writing to her, and she responded with enthusiasm.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lord Melbourne's tutoring of Victoria took place against a background of two potentially damaging political events: first, the <a href="/wiki/Lady_Flora_Hastings" title="Lady Flora Hastings">Lady Flora Hastings</a> affair, followed not long after by the <a href="/wiki/Bedchamber_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Bedchamber Crisis">Bedchamber Crisis</a>. Victoria's reputation suffered in an 1839 court intrigue when Hastings, one of her mother's ladies-in-waiting, developed an abdominal growth that was widely rumoured to be an out-of-wedlock pregnancy by <a href="/wiki/Sir_John_Conroy" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir John Conroy">Sir John Conroy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Hibbert_p._77-78_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hibbert_p._77-78-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Victoria believed the rumours, as did Lord Melbourne.<sup id="cite_ref-Hibbert_p._77-78_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hibbert_p._77-78-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> When Victoria told Melbourne of her suspicions, he planted the idea in her head that her mother, <a href="/wiki/Princess_Victoria_of_Saxe-Coburg-Saalfeld" title="Princess Victoria of Saxe-Coburg-Saalfeld">the Duchess of Kent</a>, was jealous of Hasting's closeness to Conroy, which made Victoria excited and more resolute on the matter.<sup id="cite_ref-Weintraub,_119_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Weintraub,_119-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Initially, Melbourne "suggested quiet watchfulness" over Hastings's body changes.<sup id="cite_ref-Weintraub,_119_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Weintraub,_119-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> But after the court physician, Sir James Clarke, had examined Hastings and generally concluded she wasn't pregnant, Melbourne was wholly persuaded Hastings must be pregnant from a throwaway comment that Clarke made about the appearance of virginity in spite of pregnancy. Melbourne immediately informed the queen. When Victoria observed to him that Hastings had not been seen in public for a while because "she was so sick," Melbourne "repeated, 'Sick?' with what the queen described as 'a significant laugh.<span style="padding-right:.15em;">'</span>" <sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Chartism" title="Chartism">Chartism</a> took its name from the People's Charter of 1838 and was a working-class movement for parliamentary reform. The Chartists also fought against the <a href="/wiki/Poor_Law_Amendment_Act_1834" title="Poor Law Amendment Act 1834">New Poor Law of 1834</a>. In June 1839, the petition, signed by 1.3 million working people, was presented to the House of Commons, but MPs voted not to hear the petitioners.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_affairs">Foreign affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Foreign affairs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Rebellions_of_1837%E2%80%931838" title="Rebellions of 1837–1838">Rebellions of 1837–1838</a> led directly to <a href="/wiki/John_Lambton,_1st_Earl_of_Durham" title="John Lambton, 1st Earl of Durham">Lord Durham</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Report_on_the_Affairs_of_British_North_America" class="mw-redirect" title="Report on the Affairs of British North America">Report on the Affairs of British North America</a></i> and to <a href="/wiki/Act_of_Union_1840" title="Act of Union 1840">The British North America Act, 1840</a> which established a new political entity, the <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Canada" title="Province of Canada">Province of Canada</a>.
</p><p>The Whig cabinet under Melbourne decided on 1 October 1839 to send an expeditionary force to China to protect British interests.  Such interests consisted of the British to continue to traffic opium into China, against the wishes of the Chinese under the Daoguang Emperor.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/First_Opium_War" title="First Opium War">First Opium War</a> was fought between China and the United Kingdom from 1839 to 1842, one of the outcomes of the war was that <a href="/wiki/Hong_Kong" title="Hong Kong">Hong Kong</a> would be ceded to the UK and become a British crown colony.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/First_Anglo-Afghan_War" title="First Anglo-Afghan War">First Anglo-Afghan War</a> occurred between 1839 and 1842. At the beginning of the conflict, the <a href="/wiki/East_India_Company" title="East India Company">East India Company</a> troops had defeated the forces of Afghan Emir and in 1839 occupied <a href="/wiki/Kabul" title="Kabul">Kabul</a>.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi" title="Treaty of Waitangi">Treaty of Waitangi</a> was signed on 6 February 1840 by representatives of the British Crown and <a href="/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people" title="Māori people">Māori</a> chiefs. In November 1840 a royal charter was signed by Queen Victoria, establishing <a href="/wiki/New_Zealand" title="New Zealand">New Zealand</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Crown_colony" title="Crown colony">Crown colony</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Rule_and_resign">Rule and resign</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Rule and resign">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output <figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Taking_of_Chusan_(satire).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/The_Taking_of_Chusan_%28satire%29.jpg/220px-The_Taking_of_Chusan_%28satire%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/The_Taking_of_Chusan_%28satire%29.jpg/330px-The_Taking_of_Chusan_%28satire%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/The_Taking_of_Chusan_%28satire%29.jpg/440px-The_Taking_of_Chusan_%28satire%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1079" data-file-height="759" /></a><figcaption>Satire of the <a href="/wiki/Bedchamber_crisis" title="Bedchamber crisis">Bedchamber crisis</a> by <a href="/wiki/John_Doyle_(Irish_artist)" title="John Doyle (Irish artist)">John Doyle</a>, 31 December 1840</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 7 May 1839, Melbourne announced his intention to resign, which began a series of events that led to the <a href="/wiki/Bedchamber_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Bedchamber Crisis">Bedchamber Crisis</a>. A prospective prime minister, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Peel" title="Robert Peel">Robert Peel</a>, requested that Victoria dismiss some of the wives and daughters of Whig MPs who made up her personal entourage, arguing that the monarch should avoid any hint of favouritism to a party out of power. The Queen refused to comply and was supported by Melbourne although he was unaware that Peel had not requested the resignation of all of the Queen's ladies, as she had led him to believe—and hence, Peel refused to form a new government, and Melbourne was persuaded to stay on as Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Among his government's Acts were a reduction in the number of capital offences, reforms of local government, and the <a href="/wiki/Poor_Law_Amendment_Act_1834" title="Poor Law Amendment Act 1834">reform of the Poor laws</a>. This restricted the terms on which the poor were allowed relief and established compulsory admission to <a href="/wiki/Workhouse" title="Workhouse">workhouses</a> for the impoverished.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2017)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>On 25 February 1841, Melbourne was admitted as a <a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">Fellow of the Royal Society</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following <a href="/wiki/August_1841_vote_of_no_confidence_against_the_government_of_the_Viscount_Melbourne" class="mw-redirect" title="August 1841 vote of no confidence against the government of the Viscount Melbourne">a vote of no confidence</a>, initiated by Conservative MP <a href="/wiki/John_Stuart-Wortley,_2nd_Baron_Wharncliffe" title="John Stuart-Wortley, 2nd Baron Wharncliffe">John Stuart-Wortley</a>, Melbourne's government fell, and he resigned as Prime Minister on 30 August 1841.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_life">Later life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Later life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ViscountMelbournePlaque.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/ViscountMelbournePlaque.jpg/220px-ViscountMelbournePlaque.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/ViscountMelbournePlaque.jpg/330px-ViscountMelbournePlaque.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/ViscountMelbournePlaque.jpg/440px-ViscountMelbournePlaque.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2448" data-file-height="3264" /></a><figcaption>A plaque marking the burial of Melbourne at <a href="/wiki/St_Etheldreda%27s_Church,_Hatfield" title="St Etheldreda&#39;s Church, Hatfield">St Etheldreda's Church, Hatfield</a>, in Hertfordshire, England</figcaption></figure>
<p>After Melbourne resigned permanently in August 1841, Victoria continued to write to him about political matters, but as it was deemed inappropriate after a time, their letters became cordial and non-political without issue.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeintraub1997131_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeintraub1997131-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> It has been observed that Melbourne's role faded as Victoria increasingly relied on her new husband, <a href="/wiki/Albert,_Prince_Consort" class="mw-redirect" title="Albert, Prince Consort">Prince Albert</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2017)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Though weakened, Melbourne survived a stroke on 23 October 1842, fourteen months after his departure from politics.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> In retirement, he lived at <a href="/wiki/Brocket_Hall" title="Brocket Hall">Brocket Hall</a>, Hertfordshire. He died at home on 24 November 1848<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> and was buried nearby at <a href="/wiki/St_Etheldreda%27s_Church,_Hatfield" title="St Etheldreda&#39;s Church, Hatfield">St Etheldreda's Church, Hatfield</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hertfordshire" title="Hertfordshire">Hertfordshire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> There is a memorial to him in <a href="/wiki/St_Paul%27s_Cathedral" title="St Paul&#39;s Cathedral">St Paul's Cathedral</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Upon his death, his titles passed to his brother, <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Lamb,_3rd_Viscount_Melbourne" title="Frederick Lamb, 3rd Viscount Melbourne">Frederick</a>, as his son, George Augustus Frederick (1807–1836), the only child to survive birth after Lady Caroline's two miscarriages, had predeceased him.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Melbourne" title="Melbourne">Melbourne</a>, the capital city of <a href="/wiki/Victoria,_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Victoria, Australia">Victoria, Australia</a>, was named in his honour in March 1837. He was the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-City_of_Melbourne_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-City_of_Melbourne-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mount_Melbourne" title="Mount Melbourne">Mount Melbourne</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Stratovolcano" title="Stratovolcano">stratovolcano</a> in <a href="/wiki/Antarctica" title="Antarctica">Antarctica</a>, was also named in his honour by the British <a href="/wiki/Royal_Navy" title="Royal Navy">naval officer</a> and <a href="/wiki/List_of_explorers" title="List of explorers">explorer</a> <a href="/wiki/James_Clark_Ross" title="James Clark Ross">James Clark Ross</a>, in 1841.<sup id="cite_ref-Ross-2011_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ross-2011-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_literature">In literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: In literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><a href="/wiki/Letitia_Elizabeth_Landon" title="Letitia Elizabeth Landon">Letitia Elizabeth Landon</a>'s poetical illustration <i>Lord Melbourne</i>, to a portrait by Thomas Lawrence, was published in Fisher's Drawing Room Scrap Book, 1837. It is one of the few instances in which she allowed herself a political comment.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_popular_culture">In popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: In popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li>On screen, Lord Melbourne has been portrayed by several actors:
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Rufus_Sewell" title="Rufus Sewell">Rufus Sewell</a> in the UK TV series <i><a href="/wiki/Victoria_(UK_TV_series)" class="mw-redirect" title="Victoria (UK TV series)">Victoria</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Paul_Bettany" title="Paul Bettany">Paul Bettany</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Young_Victoria" title="The Young Victoria">The Young Victoria</a></i> (2009)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Nigel_Hawthorne" title="Nigel Hawthorne">Nigel Hawthorne</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/Victoria_%26_Albert_(TV_serial)" title="Victoria &amp; Albert (TV serial)">Victoria &amp; Albert</a></i> (2001)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Joseph_O%27Conor" title="Joseph O&#39;Conor">Joseph O'Conor</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/Edward_the_Seventh" title="Edward the Seventh">Edward the Seventh</a></i> (1975)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Jon_Finch" title="Jon Finch">Jon Finch</a> in the film <i><a href="/wiki/Lady_Caroline_Lamb_(film)" title="Lady Caroline Lamb (film)">Lady Caroline Lamb</a></i> (1972)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Karl_Ludwig_Diehl" title="Karl Ludwig Diehl">Karl Ludwig Diehl</a> in the Austrian film <i><a href="/wiki/Victoria_in_Dover_(1954_film)" title="Victoria in Dover (1954 film)">Victoria in Dover</a></i> (1954)</li></ul></li></ul>
